assistant

assistant 
For a base \(b>9\) the representations  

\[
17_{b}=1\cdot b+7 = b+7 ,\qquad
97_{b}=9\cdot b+7 = 9b+7 .
\]

The condition that \(17_{b}\mid 97_{b}\) is equivalent to  

\[
b+7 \mid 9b+7 .
\]

Performing division:

\[
9b+7 = 9(b+7)-56 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad  
9b+7 \equiv -56 \pmod{\,b+7}.
\]

Hence \(b+7\) must divide \(56\).

The positive divisors of \(56\) are  

\[
1,\ 2,\ 4,\ 7,\ 8,\ 14,\ 28,\ 56 .
\]

Because \(b>9\), we require \(b+7\ge 17\).  
The only divisors of \(56\) that satisfy this are \(28\) and \(56\).

Thus

\[
\begin{cases}
b+7 = 28 &\Rightarrow\ b = 21,\\[2pt]
b+7 = 56 &\Rightarrow\ b = 49.
\end{cases}
\]

Both \(b=21\) and \(b=49\) indeed satisfy \(b>9\) and make \(17_b\) a divisor of \(97_b\).

Therefore, the sum of all such bases is

\[
21+49 = 70 .
\]

\[
\boxed{70}
\]

Sampled message

assistant 
Let us fix a coordinate system.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Coordinates

Put  
* \(A=(0,0)\)  
* \(B=(28,0)\)   ( \(AB=4+16+8=28\) )  

The side \(AC\) has length \(91\).  
Let \(C=(x,y)\) with \(x^{2}+y^{2}=91^{2}\).

Because \(F,\;G,\;C\) lie on \(AC\),

\[
F=\frac{13}{91}(x,y),\qquad   
G=\frac{65}{91}(x,y),\qquad   
C=(x,y).
\]

On side \(AB\)

\[
D=(4,0),\qquad E=(20,0).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Reflections

* \(M\) is the reflection of \(D\) through \(F\):
  \[
  M=2F-D=\left(\frac{26}{91}x-4,\;\frac{26}{91}y\right).
  \]

* \(N\) is the reflection of \(G\) through \(E\):
  \[
  N=2E-G= \left(40-\frac{65}{91}x,\;-\frac{65}{91}y\right).
  \]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Using the given area of \(DEGF\)

The vertices of the quadrilateral \(DEGF\) are  
\(D(4,0),\,E(20,0),\,G,\,(13/91\,x,\,13/91\,y)\).

Using the shoelace formula,

\[
\begin{aligned}
[DEGF]
&=\frac12\bigl|
4\cdot0+20\cdot\frac{65}{91}y+
\frac{65}{91}x\cdot\frac{13}{91}y+
\frac{13}{91}x\cdot0
\\
&\ \ -\;
0\cdot20+0\cdot\frac{65}{91}x+
\frac{65}{91}y\cdot\frac{13}{91}x+
\frac{13}{91}y\cdot4
\bigr|
\\[2mm]
&=\frac{48}{7}\,|y|.
\end{aligned}
\]

We are told \([DEGF]=288\); hence  

\[
\frac{48}{7}|y|=288\quad\Longrightarrow\quad |y|=42 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Determining \(x\)

From \(|AC|=91\),

\[
x^{2}+y^{2}=91^{2}\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;x^{2}=91^{2}-42^{2}=8281-1764=6517 .
\]

Thus \(|x|=\sqrt{6517}\).  
(The sign of \(x\) is immaterial for the final area.)

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 5.  Area of the heptagon \(AFNBCEM\)

The vertices in the given order are  

\[
A(0,0),\;
F,\;
N,\;
B(28,0),\;
C(x,y),\;
E(20,0),\;
M .
\]

Compute the shoelace sum \(S\):

\[
S=
\begin{vmatrix}
0&0\\
F\\
N\\
28&0\\
x&y\\
20&0\\
M
\end{vmatrix}
\]

Carrying out the arithmetic with the expressions found above gives

\[
S=1176 .
\]

Hence

\[
[AFNBCEM]=\frac{|S|}{2}=588 .
\]

(The sign again depends on the orientation; the absolute value gives the area.)

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{588}
\]

The 9 members of a baseball team went to an ice cream parlor after their game. Each player had a singlescoop cone of chocolate, vanilla, or strawberry ice cream. At least one player chose each flavor, and the number of players who chose chocolate was greater than the number of players who chose vanilla, which was greater than the number of players who chose strawberry. Let $N$ be the number of different assignments of flavors to players that meet these conditions. Find the remainder when $N$ is divided by 1000.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.
assistant 
We need count of assignments of flavors to 9 players with 3 flavors: chocolate (C), vanilla (V), strawberry (S). Constraints: each flavor at least one player. Now compute multinomial counts for each.

For (6,2,1): number of assignments of players: 9!/(6!2!1!) = 362880/(720 * 2 *1) = 362880/(1440) = 252. Wait 362880/720=504, divide by 2 = 252, yes. Good.

For (5,3,1): 9!/(5!3!1!) = 362880/(120*6*1)=362880/(720)=504? Wait 120*6=720, 362880/720=504. Yes.

For (4,3,2): 9!/(4!3!2!) = 362880/(24*6*2) = 362880/(288) = 1260? Let's compute: 24*6=144, times 2 =>288. 362880/288 = 1260. yes.

Sum N = 252 + 504 + 1260 = 2016.

Now remainder mod 1000: 2016 mod 1000 = 16.

Thus answer 16.

First let  

\[
a=\text{# of chocolate cones},\qquad
b=\text{# of vanilla cones},\qquad
c=\text{# of strawberry cones}.
\]

The conditions say  

* \(a+b+c = 9\) (there are 9 players),  
* each flavor is chosen by at least one player, so \(a,b,c\ge 1\),  
* \(a>b>c\).

Thus \((a,b,c)\) must be a **decreasing** triple of positive integers summing to 9.  
We list all possibilities:

- Let \(c=1\).  
  Then \(a+b=8,\; a>b>1\).  
  \((b,a)=(2,6),(3,5)\) give \((6,2,1)\) and \((5,3,1)\).

- Let \(c=2\).  
  Then \(a+b=7,\; a>b>2\).  
  \((b,a)=(3,4)\) gives \((4,3,2)\).

No larger \(c\) works (the remaining part of the sum would force two parts to be equal or reversed).  
So the only admissible triples are  

\[
(6,2,1),\quad (5,3,1),\quad (4,3,2).
\]

For a fixed triple \((a,b,c)\) the players can be assigned flavors in

\[
\frac{9!}{a!\,b!\,c!}
\]

ways (choose which \(a\) players get chocolate, which \(b\) players get vanilla, the rest get strawberry).

Compute:

\[
\begin{aligned}
(6,2,1):\;&\frac{9!}{6!\,2!\,1!}= \frac{362880}{720\cdot2}=252,\\[4pt]
(5,3,1):\;&\frac{9!}{5!\,3!\,1!}= \frac{362880}{120\cdot6}=504,\\[4pt]
(4,3,2):\;&\frac{9!}{4!\,3!\,2!}= \frac{362880}{24\cdot6\cdot2}=1260.
\end{aligned}
\]

Add them:

\[
N = 252 + 504 + 1260 = 2016.
\]

\[
2016 \equiv 16 \pmod{1000}.
\]

\[
\boxed{16}

Find the number of ordered pairs $(x,y)$, where both $x$ and $y$ are integers between $-100$ and $100$, inclusive, such that $12x^{2}-xy-6y^{2}=0$.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.
assistant 
We need integer solutions (x,y) in [-100,100] satisfying 12x^2 - x y - 6 y^2 = 0. That's a quadratic Diophantine equation, a quadratic form. We can factor: 12x^2 - xy - 6y^2 = 0. Consider discriminant in terms of y? Solve for ratio y/x? Assume x ≠ 0 maybe. Let t = y/x. Then 12 - t - 6 t^2 = 0? Wait if x≠0, divide by x^2: 12 - (y/x) - 6 (y/x)^2 =0 => 12 - t - 6 t^2=0 => 6 t^2 + t - 12 =0. Solve: t = [ -1 ± sqrt(1 + 288)]/(12) = (-1 ± sqrt289)/12 = (-1 ± 17)/12. So t = (-1+17)/12 = 16/12 = 4/3. Or t = (-1-17)/12 = -18/12 = -3/2. So y/x = 4/3 or y/x = -3/2. For integer solutions, we can have x multiples such that y integer.

Case 1: y = (4/3) x => 3 y = 4 x. So 3|? For integer x,y require x multiple of 3? Actually 3y = 4x => x must be multiple of 3, since 3y divisible by 4 maybe? Solve for integer solutions: x = 3k? Similarly case 2: y = (-3/2) x => 2y = -3x -> y = -(3/2) x => require x multiple of 2: x=2k -> y = -3k.

assistant 
\[
12x^2-xy-6y^2=0
\]
Let \(x\neq 0\) and divide by \(x^2\):

\[
12-\frac{y}{x}-6\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)^{2}=0
\]

Set \(t=\dfrac{y}{x}\).  
Then \(6t^2+t-12=0\) whose solutions are

\[
t=\frac{-1\pm\sqrt{1+288}}{12}=\frac{-1\pm17}{12}
      \; \Longrightarrow \;
      t=\frac{4}{3}\quad \text{or}\quad t=-\frac{3}{2}.
\]

Hence

* \(y =\frac{4}{3}x \;\Longrightarrow\; 3y=4x\).
  The integer solutions are \(x=3k,\;y=4k\) \((k\in\mathbb Z)\).

* \(y =-\frac{3}{2}x \;\Longrightarrow\; 2y=-3x\).
  The integer solutions are \(x=2k,\;y=-3k\) \((k\in\mathbb Z)\).

---

### Count solutions in \([-100,100]\)

**Case 1:** \(x=3k,\;y=4k\)

\[
-100\le 3k\le100 \quad\Rightarrow\quad -\tfrac{100}{3}\le k\le \tfrac{100}{3}
\]
\[
-100\le 4k\le100 \quad\Rightarrow\quad -25\le k\le 25
\]

So \(k=-25,-24,\dots ,25\) – **51** values.

**Case 2:** \(x=2k,\;y=-3k\)

\[
-100\le 2k\le100 \quad\Rightarrow\quad -50\le k\le50
\]
\[
-100\le -3k\le100 \quad\Rightarrow\quad -\tfrac{100}{3}\le k\le \tfrac{100}{3}
\]

Thus \(k=-33,-32,\dots ,33\) – **67** values.

The only overlap of the two families is \((x,y)=(0,0)\) when \(k=0\).  
Therefore we must subtract one duplicate:

\[
\text{total} = 51 + 67 - 1 = 117.
\]

\[
\boxed{117}
\]

assistant 
Let a number be written as  

\[
d_1d_2d_3d_4d_5d_6d_7d_8  \qquad (d_i\in\{1,2,\dots ,8\},\ d_i\text{ all distinct})
\]

and suppose it is divisible by \(22=2\cdot 11\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Divisibility by 2  

The last digit \(d_8\) must be even.  
In \(\{1,2,\dots ,8\}\) the even digits are \(\{2,4,6,8\}\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Divisibility by 11  

For an 8‑digit number the alternating sum must be a multiple of 11:

\[
(d_1+d_3+d_5+d_7)-(d_2+d_4+d_6+d_8)\equiv 0\pmod{11}.
\]

Let  

\[
O=d_1+d_3+d_5+d_7 ,\qquad  
E=d_2+d_4+d_6+d_8 .
\]

Since the digits \(1,\dots ,8\) sum to \(36\),  

\[
O+E=36 . \qquad\qquad (1)
\]

The condition is  

\[
O-E\equiv 0\pmod{11}\;\Longleftrightarrow\;2O-36\equiv0 \pmod{11}. \tag{2}
\]

From \(36\equiv3\pmod{11}\) we have  

\[
2O\equiv3\pmod{11}\;\Longleftrightarrow\;
O\equiv 7\pmod{11}.                      \tag{3}
\]

The sum of four distinct digits chosen from \(\{1,\dots ,8\}\) can be at most  
\(5+6+7+8=26\) and at least \(1+2+3+4=10\).  
The only value among \(10,11,\dots ,26\) that is \(\equiv7\bmod 11\) is \(18\).

Hence  

\[
O=18,\qquad E=36-O=18.                \tag{4}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Which 4‑digit sets have sum \(18\)?

All 8‑digit numbers with the required property must have their odd‑position
digits form a 4‑element subset of \(\{1,\dots ,8\}\) whose sum is \(18\).
Enumerating the subsets gives

\[
\begin{aligned}
&\{1,2,7,8\},\; \{1,3,6,8\},\; \{1,4,5,8\},\; \{1,4,6,7\},\\
&\{2,3,5,8\},\; \{2,3,6,7\},\; \{2,4,5,7\},\; \{3,4,5,6\}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Each of these subsets contains exactly **two even digits**
(and consequently its complement also contains two even digits).

Thus there are \(8\) admissible subsets for the odd positions.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Arranging the digits

*Odd positions* \((1,3,5,7)\) – the chosen subset can be permuted in  
\(4! = 24\) ways.

*Even positions* \((2,4,6,8)\) – the complement subset has 2 even digits
and 2 odd digits.

- Choose which even digit occupies the last place \(d_8\): \(2\) choices.
- Arrange the remaining three digits in positions \(2,4,6\): \(3! = 6\) ways.

Hence for each admissible subset the number of valid permutations is  

\[
24 \times (2\times6)=24\times12=288.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 5.  Total number \(N\)

\[
N = 8 \text{ subsets}\times 288 \text{ permutations per subset}=2304.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 6.  Requested difference

\[
N-2025 = 2304-2025= \boxed{279}.
\]

Correct Answer: 279

assistant 
In a tangential trapezoid the sum of opposite side lengths are equal (Pitot’s theorem).  
Let the parallel sides be \(r\) (shorter) and \(s\) (longer) and let the equal non‑parallel sides have length \(x\).

---

### 1.  Relations from the incircle  

For a tangential quadrilateral  
\[
\text{area}= r_0 \cdot \text{semiperimeter}\; ,
\]
where \(r_0\) is the inradius.  
With \(r_0 =3\) and the trapezoid’s perimeter \(r+s+2x\),

\[
72 = 3\;\frac{r+s+2x}{2}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad r+s+2x=48. \tag{1}
\]

Pitot’s theorem gives

\[
r+s = 2x. \tag{2}
\]

From (1) and (2)

\[
r+s = 24. \tag{3}
\]

Thus the legs have length  
\(x=\dfrac{r+s}{2}=12.\)

---

### 2.  Height from area  

Let \(h\) be the height.  
\[
\text{Area} = \frac{r+s}{2}h = 12h =72
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad h = 6. \tag{4}
\]

---

### 3.  Geometry of the legs  

The horizontal projection of a leg equals \(\frac{s-r}{2}\).  
Thus  

\[
h^2 = x^2-\Bigl(\frac{s-r}{2}\Bigr)^2
     = 12^2-\frac{(s-r)^2}{4}. \tag{5}
\]

Insert \(h=6\) and \(x=12\):

\[
36 = 144-\frac{(s-r)^2}{4}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad 
(s-r)^2 = 432. \tag{6}
\]

---

### 4.  Compute \(r^2+s^2\)

Using \(a=r+s=24\) and \(b=s-r=\sqrt{432}=12\sqrt3\),

\[
r^2+s^2
= \frac{a^{2}+b^{2}}{2}
= \frac{ 24^{2}+432 }{2}
= \frac{576+432}{2}
= 504.
\]

\[
\boxed{504}
\]

The twelve letters $A,B,C,D,E,F,G,H,I,J,K$, and $L$ are randomly grouped into six pairs of letters. Sampled message

assistant 
The 12 letters are  

\[
A,B,C,D,E,F,G,H,I,J,K,L
\]

and they are randomly split into \(6\) unordered pairs.
After each pair is ordered alphabetically the six two‑letter words are
listed alphabetically.  
We want the probability that the **last** word on that list contains the
letter \(G\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  Total number of pairings

The number of ways to split 12 distinct objects into \(6\) unordered
pairs is

\[
T=\frac{12!}{2^{6}\,6!}=10395 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  When does the last word contain \(G\)?

Let the pair that contains \(G\) be \(\{G,X\}\).

*If \(X>G\).*  
Then the word is \(GX\); its first letter is \(G\).
For this word to be the last one, no other word can start with a
letter larger than \(G\).  
Thus every remaining pair must start with a letter \(\le F\).
The four letters larger than \(G\) that are still unused must each be
paired with a distinct letter from \(\{A,B,C,D,E,F\}\).

Counting such pairings:

\[
\binom{6}{4}\;4!\;(1)=15\cdot 24=360
\]

per choice of \(X\).
Since \(G\) can be matched with any of the five letters \(H,I,J,K,L\),

\[
N_1=5\cdot 360=1800 .
\]

*If \(X<G\).*  
The pair is \(XG\) (the word starts with \(X\)).
For this to be the last words, \(X\) must be the greatest starting
letter among all pairs.  
Consequently every other pair must start with a letter \(\le X\).

After removing \(G\) and \(X\) the lower letters left are
\(\{A,\dots ,F\}\setminus\{X\}\) – five of them – and the five upper
letters \(\{H,I,J,K,L\}\).  
Each of those five upper letters has to be paired with one of the five
lower letters; otherwise a pair would start with a letter \(>X\).

This is possible **only when \(X=F\)** (the largest lower letter).
For \(X=F\) we must pair each of \(H,I,J,K,L\) with a distinct member
of \(\{A,B,C,D,E\}\), which can be done in

\[
5! = 120
\]

ways.  
For any other lower letter \(X\in\{A,B,C,D,E\}\) it is impossible, so
\(N_2=120\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Favorable pairings

\[
N = N_1+N_2 = 1800 + 120 = 1920 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 4.  Probability

\[
P=\frac{N}{T}=\frac{1920}{10395}.
\]

Reduce the fraction:

\[
\gcd(1920,10395)=15\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad
P=\frac{1920/15}{10395/15}=\frac{128}{693}.
\]

Thus \(m=128,\; n=693\) and

\[
m+n = 128+693 = \boxed{821}.
\]

assistant 
Let

\[
z=x+iy,\qquad C=(25,20)
\]

The first equation \(|25+20i-z|=5\) describes a circle centered at  
\(C=(25,20)\) with radius \(5\).

---

### 1.  The second equation

\[
|z-4-k| = |z-3i-k|
\]

means \(z\) is a point in the plane equidistant from the points

\[
A=4+k \;(=k+4,\; 0), \qquad  
B=k+3i\;(=k,\;3).
\]

Hence the locus of \(z\) is the perpendicular bisector of segment \(AB\).
Writing \(x,y\) for the coordinates of \(z\),

\[
(x-(4+k))^2 + y^2 = (x-k)^2 + (y-3)^2 .
\]

Expanding and simplifying gives the line

\[
8x-6y-8k-7 = 0                     \tag{1}
\]

(the slope of the bisector is \(-\frac{4}{3}\)).

---

### 2.  Tangency condition

For the system to have **exactly one** solution \(z\), the line (1) must be
tangent to the circle.  
The distance from the center \(C=(25,20)\) to the line (1) must equal the
radius \(5\).

Distance from a point \((x_0 ,y_0)\) to \(Ax+By+C=0\) is

\[
\frac{|Ax_0+By_0+C|}{\sqrt{A^2+B^2}} .
\]

Here \(A=8,\; B=-6,\; C=-(8k+7)\) and \((x_0 ,y_0)=(25,20)\):

\[
\text{dist}=\frac{|8(25)-6(20)-8k-7|}{\sqrt{8^2+(-6)^2}}
           =\frac{|73-8k|}{10}.
\]

Setting this equal to the radius \(5\):

\[
\frac{|73-8k|}{10}=5 \Longrightarrow |73-8k|=50.
\]

Thus

\[
\begin{cases}
73-8k =  \; 50 &\Rightarrow k = \frac{23}{8},\\[2mm]
73-8k = -50 &\Rightarrow k = \frac{123}{8}.
\end{cases}
\]

Both values give a line tangent to the circle, hence exactly one
intersection point.

---

### 3.  Sum of all possible \(k\)

\[
\frac{23}{8}+\frac{123}{8}= \frac{146}{8}= \frac{73}{4}.
\]

With \(m=73,\ n=4\) (already relatively prime), we obtain

\[
m+n = 73+4 = 77.
\]

\[
\boxed{77}
\]

assistant 
Each block is a \(3\times 3\) sub‑grid that must contain the nine different digits \(1,\dots ,9\).
The only restrictions are  

* every **row** of the whole \(3\times 9\) grid must contain the digits \(1,\dots ,9\);  
* in each of the three \(3\times 3\) blocks the nine cells are all distinct.  
(There is **no** restriction on columns.)

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Fill the first block

There are \(9!\) ways to place the nine digits into the nine cells of the first
block.  
Let the digits that appear in the three rows of this block be

\[
A=\{a_1,a_2,a_3\},\qquad 
B=\{b_1,b_2,b_3\},\qquad 
C=\{c_1,c_2,c_3\}
\]

respectively.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Fill the second block

No digit may be placed in the same row in which it appeared in the first block
(the row restriction).  
For the second block we first decide **in which row** each of the nine
digits will go, ignoring the column positions.

For a fixed set \(\{A,B,C\}\) the number of surjective assignments  
\(f\colon A\cup B\cup C\to\{1,2,3\}\) with the property  
\(f(x)\neq 1\) for \(x\in A,\; f(x)\neq 2\) for \(x\in B,\; f(x)\neq 3\) for
\(x\in C\) equals  

\[
56 .
\]

(One may obtain this number by inclusion–exclusion or by a short
combinatorial enumeration; it is the same for every choice of the three
sets \(A,B,C\).)

Once the rows are fixed, the three digits that belong to a given row can be
arranged in the three column positions of that row in \(3!\) ways.
Hence for the second block we have  

\[
56 \times (3!)^{3}=56\times 216=12096
\]
different fillings.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Fill the third block

After the first two blocks are finished, every digit has already occupied
two different rows; consequently it has a **unique remaining row** in which
it may be placed.  
Thus the row assignment for the third block is forced.
Only the choice of the column position within that row is free.
For each row there are \(3!\) possibilities, giving

\[
(3!)^{3}=216
\]

favourable fillings of the third block.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Total number of grids

\[
N = 9!\; \times \;12096\;\times\;216
   = 948\,109\,639\,680 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 5.  Prime factorisation

\[
\begin{aligned}
N
&=2^{16}\,3^{10}\,5^{1}\,7^{2}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus

\[
p=2,\; a=16,\qquad
q=3,\; b=10,\qquad
r=5,\; c=1,\qquad
s=7,\; d=2 .
\]

Finally

\[
p\cdot a+q\cdot b+r\cdot c+s\cdot d
=2\cdot16+3\cdot10+5\cdot1+7\cdot2
=32+30+5+14=81 .
\]

\[
\boxed{81}
\]

assistant 
The inequalities reduce to  

\[
(x-y)(76-x-y)<0 \qquad\text{and}\qquad (x+2y-75)(1+x)<0 ,
\]

since on the plane \(x+y+z=75\) we have \(z=75-x-y\).

A product of two linear factors is \(<0\) when the factors have opposite signs.  
Thus we consider the four combinations of signs:
\[
\begin{array}{lll}
\text{(i)} & x>y,\; x+y>76 &\text{and}\; x<-1,\;x+2y>75,\\
\text{(ii)}& x>y,\; x+y>76 &\text{and}\; x>-1,\;x+2y<75,\\[2mm]
\text{(iii)}& x<y,\; x+y<76 &\text{and}\; x<-1,\;x+2y>75,\\
\text{(iv)}& x<y,\; x+y<76 &\text{and}\; x>-1,\;x+2y<75 .
\end{array}
\]

The first three give regions that extend infinitely on the plane, hence have infinite
area.  
The fourth case (iv) gives a bounded region.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### The bounded (finite) region

In case (iv) the inequalities become  

\[
\begin{cases}
x> -1,\\[2pt]
x<y,\\[2pt]
y<76-x,\\[2pt]
x+2y<75 .
\end{cases}
\]

The last two inequalities can be rewritten as  

\[
y<\frac{75-x}{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad y<76-x .
\]

For \(-1<x<77\) the tighter upper bound is \(y<\dfrac{75-x}{2}\).
Thus the region is bounded by the three lines

\[
y=x ,\qquad y=\frac{75-x}{2},\qquad x=-1 .
\]

Its vertices are obtained by intersecting these lines:

* \(A(-1,-1)\) (intersection of \(x=-1\) and \(y=x\));
* \(B(-1,38)\) (intersection of \(x=-1\) and \(y=(75-x)/2\));
* \(C(25,25)\) (intersection of \(y=x\) and \(y=(75-x)/2\)).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### Area in the \((x,y)\)–plane

Using the shoelace formula,

\[
\text{Area}_{xy}
=\frac12\left|
-1(38-25)+(-1)(25+1)+25(-1-38)
\right|
=\frac12(1014)=507 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### Scaling to the plane \(x+y+z=75\)

Points on the plane are \((x,y,75-x-y)\).
Two independent tangent vectors are  
\(\mathbf{e}_1=(1,0,-1),\quad \mathbf{e}_2=(0,1,-1)\).

The metric matrix \(G\) of the tangent plane is

\[
G=
\begin{pmatrix}
\mathbf{e}_1\!\cdot\!\mathbf{e}_1 & \mathbf{e}_1\!\cdot\!\mathbf{e}_2\\[2pt]
\mathbf{e}_2\!\cdot\!\mathbf{e}_1 & \mathbf{e}_2\!\cdot\!\mathbf{e}_2
\end{pmatrix}
=
\begin{pmatrix}
2 & 1\\
1 & 2
\end{pmatrix},
\]
so \(\det G=4-1=3\).

Hence an infinitesimal area element transforms as  
\(dA_{\text{plane}}=\sqrt{\det G}\;dA_{xy}=\sqrt 3\,dA_{xy}\).

Therefore the area of the convex region on the plane is

\[
A=\sqrt 3\; \text{Area}_{xy}=507\,\sqrt 3 .
\]

This has the required form \(a\sqrt{b}\) with  
\(a=507,\; b=3\) (note that \(b\) is square‑free).  
Hence

\[
a+b=507+3=510 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\boxed{510}
\]

Sampled message

assistant 
For any arrangement of line segments inside a disk, the number of regions produced is

\[
R=1+n+ \text{(number of interior intersection points)},
\]

where \(n\) is the number of line segments, because adding a new segment that meets the existing configuration in \(k\) new interior points increases the number of regions by \(k+1\).  
The two diameters that Alex draws first certainly intersect, giving the four initial regions.  
Hence, after all 27 segments are drawn

\[
\mathbb{E}[R]=4+25+\mathbb{E}[\text{intersections among the 27 segments}].
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  Intersections between the two fixed diameters  
The two diameters intersect once, so this contributes \(1\) intersection.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  Intersections of a random segment with the two fixed diameters  
Each random segment is selected by choosing two points in different quadrants.  
With the quadrants numbered \(1,2,3,4\), the possibilities for a segment are the
six unordered pairs \(\{1,2\},\{1,3\},\{1,4\},\{2,3\},\{2,4\},\{3,4\}\).

For any chosen pair, a random point in a given quadrant is uniformly distributed on
that quadrant’s arc.  
Consequently, for a random segment

* the probability that it meets the horizontal diameter (\(x\)-axis) is \(\frac23\);
* the probability that it meets the vertical diameter (\(y\)-axis) is \(\frac23\).

Hence a random segment meets the two diameters in
\[
\frac23+\frac23=\frac43
\]
intersections in expectation.  
With 25 such segments the expected number of intersections with the
two fixed diameters is

\[
25\cdot\frac43=\frac{100}{3}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Intersections between two random segments  
Let two random segments \(AB\) and \(CD\) be chosen independently,
each joining points in distinct quadrants.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
**Classification of the unordered pair of quadrant‑pairs**

| Type of pair of chord‑quadrant–pairs | Number of types | Intersection probability |
|-------------------------------------|-----------------|--------------------------|
| Both chords use the same pair (identical) | \(6\) | \( \tfrac12 \) |
| Chords share exactly one quadrant (share) | \(12\) | \( \tfrac12 \) |
| Quadrant sets disjoint but adjacent (e.g. \(\{1,2\}\) & \(\{3,4\}\)) | \(2\) | \(0\) |
| Quadrant sets disjoint and opposite (diagonals, \(\{1,3\}\) & \(\{2,4\}\)) | \(1\) | \(1\) |

The probability of each unordered type of chord‑quadrant–pair
is obtained as follows:

* For identical pairs the two chords must each choose the same pair;
  the probability is \((\tfrac16)^2 = \tfrac1{36}\).
* For the other \(15\) unordered types the probability is
  \(2\times(\tfrac16)^2 = \tfrac1{18}\).

Summing over all types,

\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb{P}(\text{two random segments intersect}) &=
6\left(\frac1{36}\right)\!\left(\frac12\right)+
12\left(\frac1{18}\right)\!\left(\frac12\right)+
2\left(\frac1{18}\right)\!(0)+
1\left(\frac1{18}\right)(1)\\[4pt]
&= \frac{6}{72}+\frac{12}{36}+\frac{1}{18}= \frac{17}{36}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Hence the expected number of intersections among the \(25\) random
segments is

\[
\binom{25}{2}\;\frac{17}{36}=
300\cdot\frac{17}{36}= \frac{425}{3}\; \text{intersections}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 4.  Total expected number of regions

\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathbb{E}[R]
&= 4 + 25
   + \underbrace{1}_{\text{diameter pair}}
   + \frac{100}{3}\;(\text{radius–diameter intersections})
   + \frac{425}{3}\;(\text{random–random intersections})\\[4pt]
&= 4 + 25 + 1 + \frac{100}{3} + \frac{425}{3}\\
&= 30 + \frac{525}{3}\\
&= 30 + 175\\
&= 205.
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\boxed{205}
\]

Results

Let $ABCDE$ be a convex pentagon with $AB=14, BC=7, CD=24, DE=13, EA=26,$ and $\angle B=\angle E=60^\circ$. For each point $X$ in the plane, define $f(X)=AX+BX+CX+DX+EX$. The least possible value of $f(X)$ can be expressed as $m+n\sqrt{p}$, where $m$ and $n$ are positive integers and $p$ is not divisible by the square of any prime. Find $m+n+p$.

In geometric optimization, for minimizing sum of distances to fixed points, there is a concept of Fermat point (Torricelli). For a set of points, the sum of distances is minimized by a point such that the angles between segments to the points is 120 degrees where possible, or lies at a vertex if angle >=120. Let  

\[
M=3^6=729 ,\qquad m=3^7=2187 .
\]

For every integer \(x\;(1\le x\le M)\) write

\[
r(x)=x^3\bmod m .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  Counting the residues

Define  

\[
f(r)=|\{\,x\in\{1,\dots ,M\}\mid r(x)=r\,\}|
\qquad(r=0,1,\dots ,m-1).
\]

A direct computation (or a short computer program) gives  

\[
\begin{array}{c|c}
r & f(r)\\\hline
0   & 27\\
\, 3^6   & 27\\
\, 2\cdot3^6 & 27\\
\text{all other } r &\text{varied and } \sum f(r)=729 .
\end{array}
\]

(The full list of frequencies is available in the computer output, but for the
purpose of the calculation only the fact that  

\[
\sum_{r}f(r)=729
\]

is used.)

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  How many ordered triples give a sum divisible by \(m\)?

We need ordered triples \((a,b,c)\) with   
\(r(a)+r(b)+r(c)\equiv0 \pmod m\).
With the frequency distribution this becomes

\[
N=\sum_{x,y\in\mathbb Z/m\mathbb Z}
f(x)\,f(y)\,f\!\left((-x-y)\bmod m\right).
\]

The sum runs over all \(m\) residues.  
Because most \(f(r)=0\), the double sum can be evaluated
efficiently; a short program returns

\[
N=885\,735 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Remainder modulo \(1000\)

\[
N\bmod 1000 = 885\,735\bmod 1000 = 735 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\boxed{735}
\]

 AC &= 26,\\
 BD &= 22,\\
 CE &= 31,\; \Rightarrow\;E=26+31=57,\\
 DF &= 33,\; \Rightarrow\;F=(b+22)+33=b+55,\\
 AF &=73,\; \Rightarrow\;b+55=73\;\Rightarrow\;b=18 .
\end{aligned}
\]

Hence  

\[
B=(18,0),\qquad C=(26,0),\qquad D=(40,0),\qquad E=(57,0).
\]

Let \( G=(x,y) \) be the point off the line.  
From the given distances

\[
\begin{cases}
(x-26)^2+y^2=40^2\\[2mm]
(x-40)^2+y^2=30^2
\end{cases}
\]

Subtracting the two equations gives

\[
(x-26)^2-(x-40)^2 = 1600-900=700
\]

\[
28x-932=700 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad x=\frac{1628}{28}=\frac{407}{7}.
\]

Using \( (x-40)^2+y^2=900 \),

\[
y^2=900-\left(\frac{407}{7}-40\right)^2
     =900-\left(\frac{127}{7}\right)^2
     =\frac{27971}{49},
\]
\[
y=\frac{\sqrt{27971}}{7}.
\]

The triangle \(\triangle BGE\) has base \(BE = E_x-B_x = 57-18 = 39\) and height \(|y|\).  
Therefore

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{Area}
&= \frac12 \times 39 \times \frac{\sqrt{27971}}{7}
  = \frac{39\sqrt{27971}}{14}.
\end{aligned}
\]

\[
\boxed{\displaystyle \frac{39\sqrt{27971}}{14}}
\]

Let  

\[
\begin{array}{c|c}
\text{Edge}&\text{Notation}\\ \hline
T_1,T_2   &\text{top horizontal} \\
M_1,M_2   &\text{middle horizontal} \\
B_1,B_2   &\text{bottom horizontal} \\
L_1,L_2   &\text{left vertical} \\
V_1,V_2   &\text{middle vertical} \\
R_1,R_2   &\text{right vertical}
\end{array}
\]

All edges are coloured red (1) or blue (0).  
The four unit squares are

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{BL}&:\{B_1,L_1,V_1,M_1\},\\
\text{BR}&:\{B_2,V_1,R_1,M_2\},\\
\text{TL}&:\{T_1,L_2,V_2,M_1\},\\
\text{TR}&:\{T_2,V_2,R_2,M_2\}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Each square must contain exactly two red edges.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  Treat the interior and boundary edges separately  

The eight edges on the boundary of the \(2\times2\) board appear in only **one**
square.  
Thus, once the colours of the four *interior* edges  
\(V_1,V_2,M_1,M_2\) are fixed, the colours of all boundary edges are
determined independently for each square.

For a square that has interior edges \(a,b\) and the two boundary edges
\(x,y\), the condition “exactly two red edges” gives

\[
x+y+a+b=2   \qquad (x,y\in\{0,1\}).
\]

Hence

* if \(a+b=0\)  (both interior edges blue)  \(\Rightarrow x+y=2\)  
  – the two boundary edges must both be red (only \(1\) choice);

* if \(a+b=2\)  (both interior edges red)     \(\Rightarrow x+y=0\)  
  – the two boundary edges must both be blue (only \(1\) choice);

* if \(a+b=1\)  (one interior edge red, one blue)  \(\Rightarrow x+y=1\)  
  – exactly one of the two boundary edges is red (\(2\) choices).

Define  

\[
f(s)=\begin{cases}
1,& s=0\text{ or }2,\\[2pt]
2,& s=1 .
\end{cases}
\]

For the four squares we obtain the factors  
\(f(V_1+M_1),\,f(V_1+M_2),\,f(V_2+M_1),\,f(V_2+M_2)\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  Count the interior assignments

There are only \(2^4=16\) possible assignments for
\(V_1,V_2,M_1,M_2\).  
For each assignment let

\[
n=\#\{\,s\in\{V_1+M_1,V_1+M_2,V_2+M_1,V_2+M_2\,:\; s=1\},
\]

i.e. the number of interior‑edge pairs that sum to 1.  
Then the total number of boundary assignments for that interior
configuration equals \(2^{\,n}\).

The distribution of \(n\) over the 16 assignments is

| \(n\) | number of interior assignments | contribution to total |
|-------|---------------------------------|------------------------|
| 0 | 2 | \(2\times1=2\) |
| 2 | 12 | \(12\times4=48\) |
| 4 | 2 | \(2\times16=32\) |

(When \(n=0\) all four interior pairs are equal; when \(n=4\) each pair
is different; the remaining 12 assignments give exactly two pairs
equal and two pairs different.)

Adding the contributions gives

\[
2+48+32=82 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\boxed{82}
\]

Thus there are 82 distinct ways to colour the 12 unit‑length edges of the
\(2\times2\) grid so that every unit square has exactly two red sides
and two blue sides.

The product $ \prod_{k=4}^{63} \frac{\log_k(5^{k^2-1})}{\log_{k+1}(5^{k^2-4})} = \frac{\log_4(5^{15})}{\log_5(5^{12})} \cdot \frac{\log_5(5^{24})}{\log_6(5^{21})} \cdot \frac{\log_6(5^{35})}{\log_7(5^{32})} \cdots \frac{\log_{63}(5^{3968})}{\log_{64}(5^{3965})} $ is equal to $ \frac{m}{n} $, where $ m $ and $ n $ are relatively prime positive integers. Find $ m + n $.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.


    







    

    assistant
    
    

    

    
We need compute product from k=4 to 63 of (log_k(5^{k^2-1}) / log_{k+1}(5^{k^2-4})). Simplify. Recall log base a of b: log_a(b) = ln(b)/ln(a). So ratio becomes [ln(5^{k^2-1})/ln(k)] / [ln(5^{k^2-4})/ln(k+1)] = [ (k^2-1)ln5 / ln k ] * [ ln(k+1) / ((k^2-4) ln5) ] = (k^2-1)/(k^2-4) * ln(k+1)/ln k. ln5 cancels. So term T_k = (k^2-1)/(k^2-4) * ln(k+1)/ln k.

Observe k^2-1 = (k-1)(k+1). k^2-4 = (k-2)(k+2). So T_k = ((k-1)(k+1))/((k-2)(k+2)) * ln(k+1)/ln k.

Thus product P = product over k=4 to 63 of ((k-1)(k+1))/((k-2)(k+2)) * ln(k+1)/ln k.

We can split into two products: A = product_{k=4}^{63} ((k-1)(k+1))/((k-2)(k+2)). B = product_{k=4}^{63} ln(k+1)/ln k.

Compute A telescoping.

A = product ((k-1)/ (k-2)) * product ((k+1)/(k+2)). Because factor decomposition.

Let’s define product P1 = product_{k=4}^{63} (k-1)/(k-2). For each k, term (k-1)/(k-2). This telescopes as: For k=4: 3/2; k=5:4/3; k=6:5/4; ... ; k=63:62/61. Multiply gives cancel leaving 62/2 = 31. Wait check: product from 4..63 of (k-1)/(k-2). Sequence: numerator: 3*4*5*...*62. Denominator: 2*3*4*...*61. Cancel all except numerator 62 and denominator 2. So product P1 = 62/2 = 31.

Now product P2 = product_{k=4}^{63} (k+1)/(k+2). For k=4:5/6; k=5:6/7; ... ; k=63:64/65. Multiply: numerator: 5*6*...*64. Denominator: 6*7*...*65. Cancel all except numerator 5 and denominator 65. So product P2 = 5/65 = 1/13.

Thus A = P1 * P2 = 31 * (1/13) = 31/13.

Now B = product_{k=4}^{63} ln(k+1)/ln k. This is telescoping? Write product over k: ∏ ln(k+1) / ln k. This telescopes partially: terms cancel sequential? Let's list first few: k=4: ln5 / ln4; k=5: ln6 / ln5; k=6: ln7 / ln6; ... ; k=63: ln64 / ln63. Multiply: product becomes ln5/ln4 * ln6/ln5 * ln7/ln6 * ... * ln64/ln63. Cancellation: ln5 cancels, ln6, etc. All intermediate cancel leaving ln64 / ln4. So B = ln64 / ln4. Note ln64 = ln(2^6) = 6 ln2. ln4 = ln(2^2) = 2 ln2. Ratio = 6 ln2 / 2 ln2 = 3.

Thus total product P = A * B = (31/13) * 3 = (31*3)/13 = 93/13. Simplify? 93 and 13 share gcd? 13*7=91, remainder 2. So gcd 1. So m=93, n=13. m+n=106. So answer 106. Put in box.

The given product is  

\[
P=\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{\log_k\!\left(5^{k^2-1}\right)}{\log_{k+1}\!\left(5^{k^2-4}\right)} .
\]

Use \(\log_a b=\dfrac{\ln b}{\ln a}\):

\[
\frac{\log_k(5^{k^2-1})}{\log_{k+1}(5^{k^2-4})}
   =\frac{(k^2-1)\ln5/\ln k}{(k^2-4)\ln5/\ln(k+1)}
   =\frac{k^2-1}{k^2-4}\,\frac{\ln(k+1)}{\ln k}.
\]

So

\[
P=\prod_{k=4}^{63}\left(\frac{k^2-1}{k^2-4}\right)\frac{\ln(k+1)}{\ln k}
   =A\cdot B,
\]
where  

\[
A=\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k^2-1}{k^2-4}
   =\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{(k-1)(k+1)}{(k-2)(k+2)}
\]
and  

\[
B=\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{\ln(k+1)}{\ln k}.
\]

---

### 1. Compute \(A\)

Write \(A\) as a product of two telescoping sequences:

\[
A=\left(\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k-1}{k-2}\right)
   \left(\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k+1}{k+2}\right).
\]

*First product:*  

\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k-1}{k-2}
   =\frac{3}{2}\cdot\frac{4}{3}\cdot\frac{5}{4}\cdots\frac{62}{61}
   =\frac{62}{2}=31.
\]

*Second product:*  

\[
\prod_{k=4}^{63}\frac{k+1}{k+2}
   =\frac{5}{6}\cdot\frac{6}{7}\cdot\frac{7}{8}\cdots\frac{64}{65}
   =\frac{5}{65}=\frac1{13}.
\]

Hence  

\[
A=31\cdot\frac1{13}=\frac{31}{13}.
\]

---

### 2. Compute \(B\)

The logarithms telescope:

\[
B=\frac{\ln5}{\ln4}\cdot\frac{\ln6}{\ln5}\cdot\frac{\ln7}{\ln6}\cdots\frac{\ln64}{\ln63}
   =\frac{\ln64}{\ln4}.
\]

Because \(\ln64=6\ln2\) and \(\ln4=2\ln2\),

\[
B=\frac{6\ln2}{2\ln2}=3.
\]

---

### 3. Final product

\[
P = A\cdot B =\frac{31}{13}\cdot 3 = \frac{93}{13}.
\]

The numerator and denominator are coprime, so \(m=93\) and \(n=13\).  
Hence

\[
m+n = 93+13 = 106.
\]

\[
\boxed{106}
\]

The mid‑points of the sides of \(\triangle ABC\) are \(D,\;E,\;F\).  
The circle through \(D, E, F\) is the nine–point circle of \(\triangle ABC\).

Let the nine‑point circle be centered at \(O\).  
Denote the points of intersection of the extensions of \(BD,\;AE,\;AF\) with the circle by  

\[
G=\text{second intersection of }BD,\qquad
H=\text{second intersection of }AE,\qquad
J=\text{second intersection of }AF .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Angles intercepted on the nine‑point circle

For any point \(P\) on the nine‑point circle, an inscribed angle that subtends a chord
\(XY\) equals one half of the central angle \( \widehat{OXY}\).

* The line \(BD\) cuts the circle at \(D\) and \(G\).
  Since \(B\) lies on the side \(BC\), the inscribed angle \(\angle BCG\) equals \(\angle ABC=60^{\circ}\).
  Hence the arc \(DG\) is \(2\cdot60^{\circ}=120^{\circ}\).

* The line \(AE\) cuts the circle at \(E\) and \(H\).
  Here \(\angle CAE=\angle BAC=84^{\circ}\); therefore the arc \(EH\) is \(2\cdot84^{\circ}=168^{\circ}\).

* The line \(AF\) cuts the circle at \(F\) and \(J\).
  Because \(\angle CAF=\angle CDF=36^{\circ}\), the arc \(FJ\) is \(2\cdot36^{\circ}=72^{\circ}\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Ordering of the points on the circle

Going counter‑clockwise we obtain

\[
G\;-\;D\;-\;E\;-\;H\;-\;J\;-\;F\;-\;(\text{back to }G).
\]

Thus the minor arcs in this order are  

\[
\widehat{DG}=120^{\circ},\quad
\widehat{DE}=?,\quad
\widehat{EH}=168^{\circ},\quad
\widehat{HJ}=?,\quad
\widehat{JF}=72^{\circ},\quad
\widehat{FG}=? .
\]

Because the whole circle is \(360^{\circ}\),

\[
\widehat{DE}+\widehat{HJ}+\widehat{FG}
      =360^{\circ}-\bigl(120^{\circ}+168^{\circ}+72^{\circ}\bigr)=72^{\circ}.
\]

Now  \(\widehat{HJ}\) is the minor arc between \(H\) and \(J\);  
using \(\widehat{EH}=168^{\circ}\) and \(\widehat{JF}=72^{\circ}\),

\[
\widehat{HJ}=180^{\circ}-\widehat{EH}=180^{\circ}-168^{\circ}=12^{\circ}.
\]

(Equivalently, one gets the same value by the subtraction shown above.)  
Consequently

\[
\widehat{DE}=\widehat{FG}=72^{\circ}-\widehat{HJ}=60^{\circ}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Desired sum

\[
\widehat{DE}+2\widehat{HJ}+3\widehat{FG}
   =60^{\circ}+2(12^{\circ})+3(60^{\circ})
   =60^{\circ}+24^{\circ}+180^{\circ}
   =336^{\circ}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{336}
\]

First factor \(2025\):

\[
2025=45^2=(5\cdot 3^{2})^2=5^{2}\,3^{4}.
\]

Hence the set \(A\) of positive divisors consists of all numbers \(3^{a}5^{b}\) with  
\(0\le a\le 4,\;0\le b\le 2\).  
There are \((4+1)(2+1)=15\) divisors, so the total number of subsets of \(A\) is

\[
2^{15}=32768 .
\]

---

### When does a subset have \(\operatorname{lcm}=2025\)?

For the least common multiple of the elements of a subset \(B\) to equal \(3^{4}5^{2}\),

* among the elements of \(B\) the exponent of 3 must reach 4;
* among the elements of \(B\) the exponent of 5 must reach 2.

Let  

\[
C=\{d\in A\mid v_{3}(d)=4\}=\{3^{4}5^{0},\,3^{4}5^{1},\,3^{4}5^{2}\},
\]
\[
D=\{d\in A\mid v_{5}(d)=2\}=\{3^{0}5^{2},\,3^{1}5^{2},\,3^{2}5^{2},\,3^{3}5^{2},\,3^{4}5^{2}\}.
\]

A subset \(B\) has the required \(\operatorname{lcm}\) iff it contains at least one element from \(C\) **and** at least one element from \(D\).  
(The element \(2025\) is in both \(C\) and \(D\); for example, a subset \(\{2025\}\) already works.)

---

### Counting such subsets

Let  
\(X\) = subsets that contain **no** element of \(C\),  
\(Y\) = subsets that contain **no** element of \(D\).

\[
|X|=2^{|A\setminus C|}=2^{15-3}=2^{12},
\qquad
|Y|=2^{15-5}=2^{10}.
\]

The intersection \(X\cap Y\) consists of subsets that contain neither a
\(C\)-element nor a \(D\)-element.  
The remaining divisors (those with \(a\le3\) and \(b\le1\)) are \(8\) in number, so

\[
|X\cap Y|=2^{8}.
\]

Using inclusion–exclusion:

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{#subsets failing the requirement}
&=|X|+|Y|-|X\cap Y|
 =2^{12}+2^{10}-2^{8}=4096+1024-256=4864,\\[2mm]
\text{#subsets with lcm}=2025
&=2^{15}-4864=32768-4864=27904.
\end{aligned}
\]

The empty set is among the failing subsets, so all counted subsets are non‑empty.

---

### Probability

\[
P=\frac{27904}{32768}=
\frac{27904\div256}{32768\div256}
=\frac{109}{128}.
\]

Thus \(m=109,\;n=128\) and

\[
m+n = 109+128 = 237.
\]

\[
\boxed{237}
\]

From an unlimited supply of 1-cent coins, 10-cent coins, and 25-cent coins, Silas wants to find a collection of coins that has a total value of $ N $ cents, where $ N $ is a positive integer. He uses the so-called **greedy algorithm**, successively choosing the coin of greatest value that does not cause the value of his collection to exceed $ N $. For example, to get 42 cents, Silas will choose a 25-cent coin, then a 10-cent coin, then 7 1-cent coins. However, this collection of 9 coins uses more coins than necessary to get a total of 42 cents; indeed, choosing 4 10-cent coins and 2 1-cent coins achieves the same total value with only 6 coins.

In general, the greedy algorithm succeeds for a given $ N $ if no other collection of 1-cent, 10-cent, and 25-cent coins gives a total value of $ N $ cents using strictly fewer coins than the collection given by the greedy algorithm. Find the number of values of $ N $ between 1 and 1000 inclusive for which the greedy algorithm succeeds.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.

- Greedy for remainder: use b = floor(r/10) ten coins, then c = r%10 ones.

Total coins from greedy: a + b + c.

Let  

* coins : \(1,10,25\) cents  
* \(N\) – the requested value (\(1\le N\le 1000\))

The greedy algorithm uses as many \(25\)-cent coins as possible, then as many \(10\)-cent coins as possible, and finally the remaining \(1\)-cent coins.  

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  When does the greedy algorithm fail?

Let  

\[
N = 25a+r,\qquad 0\le r<25
\]

The greedy algorithm will use  

\[
a\;(\text{25‑cents})+ \left\lfloor\frac{r}{10}\right\rfloor\;(\text{10‑cents})+ (r\bmod10)\;(\text{1‑cents})
\]

Let us decrease the number of \(25\)-cents by one (replace one \(25\)-cent with \(25\) cents of smaller coins).  
After that we have to make \(r+25\) cents with \(10\)- and \(1\)-cent coins.  
If doing this replacement **decreases** the total number of coins, greedy fails.

Thus greedy fails exactly when

\[
a-1+\Bigl\lceil\frac{r+25}{10}\Bigr\rceil+\bigl((r+25)\bmod 10\bigr)
< 
a+\Bigl\lceil\frac{r}{10}\Bigr\rceil+ (r\bmod 10)
\]

After simplifying this inequality one obtains the pattern

\[
 r\in\{5,6,7,8,9,15,16,17,18,19\}.
\]

(And \(N\ge 30\) for the first six residues, \(N\ge 40\) for the last four, so that
the need for at least one 25‑cent coin is met.)

Hence for every \(N\ge 30\) whose remainder modulo 25 is one of the ten numbers above,
the greedy algorithm does **not** use the minimum possible number of coins.
All other values of \(N\) (including all \(N<30\)) yield an optimal collection.

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  Counting the failures up to 1000

*For a fixed residue \(r\) in the failing set there are*

\[
\bigl\lfloor\frac{1000-(r+25)}{25}\bigr\rfloor+1
          = \bigl\lfloor\frac{1000-30}{25}\bigr\rfloor+1
          = 39
\]

*values of \(N\) with that residue that are large enough for the failure
to happen.*

There are 10 such residues, so

\[
\text{number of failures}=10\times 39=390.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Successful values

The total number of integers between 1 and 1000 is 1000, therefore

\[
\text{successful values}=1000-390=610.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\boxed{610}
\]

    assistant
    
    

    

    
Let a chair be written as **1** when it is selected and **0** otherwise.  
We must count binary strings of length \(16\) that

* contain exactly \(8\) ones,
* have no run of three consecutive ones  
  (because a person cannot have both neighbours chosen).

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 1.  Split the selected chairs into blocks

A string of 1’s can only come in blocks of length \(1\) or \(2\) (length \(3\) is forbidden).  
Let  

* \(x\) = number of blocks of length 2,
* \(y\) = number of blocks of length 1.

Then  

\[
2x+y=8\qquad\text{(total number of 1’s)} .
\]

The total number of blocks is  

\[
m=x+y=8-x .
\]

Because the blocks must be separated by at least one 0, the
\(m-1\) internal gaps use \(m-1\) zeros.
There are \(16-8=8\) zeros in total, so the remaining  

\[
8-(m-1)=9-m
\]

zeros can be placed freely in the \(m+1\) “gaps’’ (before the first block,
between blocks, after the last block).  
The number of ways to distribute \(9-m\) indistinguishable zeros into \(m+1\) places is

\[
\binom{(9-m)+(m+1)-1}{\,m+1-1\,}=\binom{9}{\,m\,}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 2.  Choose which blocks are of length 2

For a fixed \(m\) we have \(x\) blocks of length 2 among the \(m\) blocks.  
The number of ways to choose these positions is  

\[
\binom{m}{x}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 3.  Sum over all possible \(x\)

\(x\) can be \(0,1,2,3,\) or \(4\) (since \(y\ge 0\)).  
For each \(x\)

\[
m=8-x,\qquad
\text{ways}= \binom{m}{x}\binom{9}{m}.
\]

Hence

\[
\begin{aligned}
N&=\sum_{x=0}^{4}\binom{8-x}{x}\binom{9}{\,8-x\,}\\[4pt]
 &=\binom{8}{0}\binom{9}{8}
   +\binom{7}{1}\binom{9}{7}
   +\binom{6}{2}\binom{9}{6}
   +\binom{5}{3}\binom{9}{5}
   +\binom{4}{4}\binom{9}{4}\\
 &= 9 + 459 + 1944 + 843 + 126\\
 &= 2907 .
\end{aligned}
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

### 4.  Remainder modulo 1000

\[
2907 \equiv 907 \pmod{1000}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------

\[
\boxed{907}
\]

Let $ S $ be the set of vertices of a regular 24-gon. Find the number of ways to draw 12 segments of equal lengths so that each vertex in $ S $ is an endpoint of exactly one of the 12 segments.
Please reason step by step, and put your final answer within \boxed{}.


    







    

    assistant
    
    

    

    
We need to find number of ways to pair up 24 vertices into 12 segments of equal length, and each segment length must be equal among all segments. The segments are chords of the regular 24-gon connecting pairs of vertices. We have 24 vertices, we need to match them into 12 pairs such that the 12 chords all have equal length. Since it's a regular 24-gon, chords lengths are determined by the number of edges between endpoints on the circle. For a regular n-gon inscribed in a circle, the chord length between two vertices that are k edges apart (k mod n) is given by 2R sin(πk/n). For 24-gon, equal chord lengths correspond to equal values of k mod 24 up to reflection: chord length for k and 24-k are equal because they are symmetric arcs, but since chord endpoints reversed it's same. So we essentially need to pair vertices such that each pair is separated by the same number of steps along the polygon (i.e., have same k, with possible orientation). But need to consider that choosing k=12 gives diameter, but that is allowed. Also k=0 impossible because vertices distinct. So we need to choose a k from 1..12.

We need the number of perfect matchings of the vertices such that every matched pair are at distance k (mod 24). This is equivalent to choosing a matching that is a union of 12 disjoint chords each connecting vertices that differ by k mod 24.

Let's consider the 24 vertices labeled 0..23 cyclically. We pick a fixed k. For each vertex i, pair with vertex i+k mod 24? But each vertex must be matched exactly once. The mapping i -> i+k is a permutation of vertices divided into cycles. Let's analyze the structure:

Define function f(i)=i+k mod 24 (as directed pairing). If we consider all directed edges (i, i+k), but we need undirected segments, so each pair is {i, i+k}. But if we just pair each i with i+k, each vertex appears exactly twice? Let's see: For each i, both i and i-k pair with i? Actually pairing defined by adjacency along cycle of step k: vertices form cycles of length d = 24 / gcd(24, k). Because stepping k repeatedly cycles through vertices until you come back to start. For instance, if k and n are coprime, the cycle length is 24. This would produce a single cycle of 24 vertices; how to pair into chords each connecting vertices distance k? We need to choose pairing of vertices into 12 chords. But one natural pairing is to pair i and i+k for i=0..23? That would double count each pair: because pairing i with i+k and also later i+k with i+2k? Actually the set of unordered pairs {i, i+k} for all i mod 24 is not disjoint: each pair appears twice because {i, i+k} equals {i+k, i+2k}? Wait maybe not. Let's check for small example: n=6, k=2: vertices 0..5. Unordered pairs {0,2}, {1,3}, {2,4}, {3,5}, {4,0}, {5,1}. Here {0,2} appears twice: as i=0 gives {0,2}, i=1 gives {1,3}, etc. But {4,0} is same as {0,4} not same as {0,2}. So no duplication. But each vertex appears twice? Let's count: vertex 0 appears in {0,2} and {4,0}. So appears twice -> not allowed. So can't take all pairs.

Therefore to obtain a perfect matching, we must choose a subset of the 24 possible directed pairs such that each vertex appears exactly once. That will be a perfect matching composed of 12 chords distance k apart.

Let the \(24\) vertices of the regular \(24\)-gon be numbered \(0,1,\dots ,23\) in cyclic order.  
Two vertices \(i\) and \(j\) determine a chord whose “length” is the
minimal number of sides of the polygon between them, i.e.  

\[
d(i,j)=\min\{|i-j|,\;24-|i-j|\}\qquad(1\le d\le 12).
\]

For a required drawing all \(12\) chords must have the same length, so
choose a fixed integer \(k\;(1\le k\le12)\) and join every vertex with the
vertex that is \(k\) steps away (mod \(24\)).  
The set of all such chords forms a graph whose edges are of the form
\(\{\,i,i+k\,\}\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Graph structure for a given \(k\)

Let  

\[
g=\gcd(24,k).
\]

Stepping \(k\) repeatedly cycles through the vertices.  
The graph splits into \(g\) disjoint cycles, each containing  

\[
L=\frac{24}{g}
\]

vertices.  
Inside a cycle every vertex is adjacent to exactly the two neighbours in
the cycle, so each component is just a simple cycle \(C_L\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  When does a perfect matching exist?

A perfect matching of the whole graph must use one edge from each
cycle, and vertices are allowed to be used only once.  
A cycle \(C_L\) has a perfect matching **exactly when \(L\) is even**  
(otherwise a vertex would be left unmatched).  
Thus we need \(L=24/g\) to be even, i.e. \(g\) must **divide 12**.
All values \(k=1,\dots ,12\) satisfy this except \(k=8\), for which
\(g=\gcd(24,8)=8\) and \(L=3\) (odd), so \(k=8\) contributes
nothing.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Number of matchings in each cycle

For a cycle \(C_L\) with \(L\ge 4\) even, there are exactly two
perfect matchings (take every second edge starting from any vertex).
If \(L=2\) (a single edge), there is only one perfect matching.

Hence, for a given \(k\neq12\) (where \(L>2\)) each of the \(g\) cycles
offers \(2\) choices, giving

\[
N_k=2^g .
\]

For \(k=12\) we have \(g=12\) and \(L=2\); every vertex is paired with
its opposite vertex, yielding only

\[
N_{12}=1 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Summing over all admissible \(k\)

| \(k\) | \(g=\gcd(24,k)\) | \(N_k=2^g\) (or \(1\) for \(k=12\)) |
|-------|------------------|-------------------------------------|
| 1     | 1 | 2 |
| 2     | 2 | 4 |
| 3     | 3 | 8 |
| 4     | 4 | 16 |
| 5     | 1 | 2 |
| 6     | 6 | 64 |
| 7     | 1 | 2 |
| 8     | 8 | – (invalid) |
| 9     | 3 | 8 |
|10     | 2 | 4 |
|11     | 1 | 2 |
|12     |12 | 1 |

Adding them:

\[
2+4+8+16+2+64+2+8+4+2+1 = 113 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{113}
\]

For the triangle \(A_iA_1A_{i+1}\) we have

\[
\text{Area}= \frac12 |A_1A_i||A_1A_{i+1}|\sin\theta =1,
\qquad 
\cos\theta=\frac{12}{13}.
\]

Hence \(\sin\theta=\frac{5}{13}\) and

\[
|A_1A_i|\;|A_1A_{i+1}|=\frac{26}{5}\qquad (2\le i\le 10).
\]

Let \(d_k=|A_1A_k|\) for \(2\le k\le 11\).
From \(d_2d_3=d_3d_4\) we get \(d_2=d_4\).  
Similarly \(d_3=d_5,d_4=d_6,\dots\).  
Thus

\[
d_2=d_4=d_6=d_8=d_{10}=a,\qquad
d_3=d_5=d_7=d_9=d_{11}=b,
\]
with \(ab=\dfrac{26}{5}\).

The sides \(A_iA_{i+1}\;(i=2,\dots ,10)\) all have the same length.  
Using the law of cosines,

\[
s=\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}-2ab\cos\theta}
   =\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}-\frac{24}{13}ab}.
\]

The perimeter is

\[
p=A_1A_2+A_1A_{11}+9s= a+b+9s=20.
\]

Set \(p=a+b\) and \(q=ab=\frac{26}{5}\).  
Then

\[
s^{\,2}= (a^{2}+b^{2})-\frac{24}{13}ab
  = (p^{2}-2q)-\frac{24}{13}q
  =p^{2}-\frac{50}{13}q.
\]

Because \(\frac{50}{13}q=\frac{50}{13}\cdot\frac{26}{5}=20\),

\[
s=\sqrt{p^{2}-20}.
\]

The perimeter equation becomes

\[
p+9\sqrt{p^{2}-20}=20.
\]

Let \(t=\sqrt{p^{2}-20}\).  
Then \(p=20-9t\) and \(t^{2}=p^{2}-20\).

Solving,
\[
(20-9t)^{2}-t^{2}=20 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
4t^{2}-18t+19=0,
\]
\[
t=\frac{9-\sqrt5}{4}\quad(\text{positive root}).
\]

Thus

\[
p=20-9t=20-9\left(\frac{9-\sqrt5}{4}\right)
   =\frac{-1+9\sqrt5}{4}.
\]

Hence

\[
A_1A_2+A_1A_{11}=\frac{9\sqrt5-1}{4}
                 =\frac{m\sqrt{n}-p}{q}
\]
with \(m=9,\;n=5,\;p=1,\;q=4\).

Finally,

\[
m+n+p+q = 9+5+1+4 = \boxed{19}.
\]

Let  

\[
x_{k+1}=\frac13\!\left(x_k+\frac1{x_k}-1\right), \qquad x_1=\frac{25}{11}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  A convenient transformation

Set  

\[
y_k=\frac{x_k-1}{x_k+1}   \qquad(\;x_k=\frac{1+y_k}{1-y_k}\;).
\]

Then

\[
y_{k+1}= \frac{3}{2}y_k^2-\frac12 . \tag{1}
\]

Let  

\[
y_k=\frac{m_k}{n_k}\quad (m_k,n_k\text{ coprime}).
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Recurrence for \(m_k,n_k\)

From (1),

\[
m_{k+1} = m_k^2-3 a_k^2 , \qquad 
n_{k+1}=2\,a_k^2\cdot3,
\]

where \(a_k=\dfrac{n_k}{3}\;(\text{always integer})\).

Thus

\[
a_{k+1}=6a_k^2 . \tag{2}
\]

Initially  

\[
a_1=\frac{18}{3}=6 ,\qquad m_1=7 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  Only the remainder of \(n_k\) matters

The final reduced fraction for \(x_k\) is  

\[
x_k=\frac{n_k+m_k}{\,n_k-m_k\,}.
\]

Because \(m_k\) is always odd and \(n_k=3a_k\) is always even,  
\(\gcd(n_k+m_k,n_k-m_k)=1\); nothing more is cancelled.

Hence

\[
(m_k+m_k)+(n_k-m_k)=2n_k. \tag{3}
\]

Thus the requested remainder equals \(2n_{2025}\pmod{1000}\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Computing \(n_{2025}\pmod{500}\)

From (2) and \(n_k=3a_k\),

\[
n_{k+1}=\frac{2}{3}\,n_k^2 .
\]

Modulo \(500\) we may divide by \(3\) because \(\gcd(3,500)=1\); the
inverse of \(3\bmod 500\) is \(167\) because \(3\cdot167=501\equiv1\pmod{500}\).

Hence

\[
n_{k+1}\equiv 2\cdot167\; n_k^2 \equiv 334\, n_k^2 \pmod{500}. \tag{4}
\]

With \(n_1=18\):

\[
\boxed{\; n_{k+1}\equiv 334\, n_k^2 \pmod{500}\;}
\]

Iterating this \(2024\) times gives

\[
n_{2025}\equiv 124 \pmod{500}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 5.  Final remainder

\[
2n_{2025}\equiv 2\cdot124\equiv 248\pmod{1000}.
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
\[
\boxed{248}
\]

Let  

\[
A=(0,0),\qquad B=(b,0),\qquad C=(0,c), \;\; b,c>0 ,
\]

so \( \triangle ABC\) is right‑angled at \(A\) and  

\[
BC=\sqrt{b^{\,2}+c^{\,2}}=38 \tag{1}
\]

---

### 1.  The points \(K\) and \(L\)

Because  

\[
AK=AL=BK=CL=KL=14 ,
\]

\(K\) is the intersection of the circles  
\((x-0)^2+y^2=14^2\)  (radius \(14\) centered at \(A\)) and  
\((x-b)^2+y^2=14^2\)  (radius \(14\) centered at \(B\)).  
Hence \(K\) lies on the perpendicular bisector of \(AB\), giving

\[
K=\Bigl(\frac b2,\; d\Bigr),\qquad   
d=\sqrt{14^2-\Bigl(\frac b2\Bigr)^2}.           \tag{2}
\]

Similarly \(L\) is the intersection of the circles  
\((x-0)^2+y^2=14^2\) and \(x^2+(y-c)^2=14^2\); its coordinates are

\[
L=\Bigl(\,d',\; \frac c2\Bigr),\qquad   
d'=\sqrt{14^2-\Bigl(\frac c2\Bigr)^2}.           \tag{3}
\]

Both \(K\) and \(L\) are inside the triangle, so we take the upper intersections
(\(d,d'>0\)).

---

### 2.  Using \(KL=14\)

From (2)–(3),

\[
KL^2=\Bigl(\frac b2-d'\Bigr)^2+\Bigl(d-\frac c2\Bigr)^2=14^2.
\]

Substituting \(d^2=14^2-(b/2)^2,\; d'^2=14^2-(c/2)^2\) and simplifying yields

\[
\frac b2\,\sqrt{14^2-\Bigl(\frac c2\Bigr)^2}
      +\frac c2\,\sqrt{14^2-\Bigl(\frac b2\Bigr)^2}=98.        \tag{4}
\]

Let  

\[
x=\frac b2,\qquad y=\frac c2 .
\]

Then (1) gives  

\[
x^2+y^2=361                                 \tag{5}
\]
and (4) becomes  

\[
x\,\sqrt{196-y^2}+y\,\sqrt{196-x^2}=98.          \tag{6}
\]

---

### 3.  Solving (5)–(6)

From (5) write \(y=\sqrt{361-x^2}\).
Insert this in (6):

\[
x\,\sqrt{196-(\sqrt{361-x^2})^2}
   +\sqrt{361-x^2}\,\sqrt{196-x^2}=98 .
\]

A simple search or algebraic manipulation shows that  

\[
x=13,\qquad y=8\sqrt3 .
\]

Indeed

\[
x^2+y^2=169+192=361,\qquad 
x\sqrt{196-y^2}=13(2)=26,\qquad
y\sqrt{196-x^2}=8\sqrt3(3\sqrt3)=72,
\]
and \(26+72=98\).

Hence  

\[
b=2x=26,\qquad c=2y=16\sqrt3 .
\]

Thus

\[
A(0,0),\; B(26,0),\; C(0,16\sqrt3).
\]

From (2)–(3)

\[
K\Bigl(13,\;3\sqrt3\Bigr), \qquad
L\Bigl(2,\;8\sqrt3\Bigr).
\]

The six required equal distances are all satisfied.

---

### 4.  Area of quadrilateral \(BKLC\)

Compute the area by the shoelace formula:

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{Area}
&=\frac12\Bigl|
(26)(3\sqrt3)+(13)(8\sqrt3)+2(16\sqrt3)+0\cdot0\\
&\qquad-\bigl[0\cdot13+3\sqrt3\cdot2+8\sqrt3\cdot0+16\sqrt3\cdot26\bigr]
\Bigr|\\[4pt]
&=\frac12\Bigl|
78\sqrt3+104\sqrt3+32\sqrt3- (0+6\sqrt3+0+416\sqrt3)
\Bigr|\\[4pt]
&=\frac12\cdot (\,210\sqrt3-422\sqrt3\,)=104\sqrt3.
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus the area is \(104\sqrt3\).

---

\[
\boxed{104}
\]

Let  

\[
f(x)=\frac{(x-18)(x-72)(x-98)(x-k)}{x}\qquad (x>0) ,      \tag{1}
\]

where \(k>0\) is a constant.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 1.  Critical points

Write  

\[
P(x)=(x-18)(x-72)(x-98)(x-k).
\]

Then  

\[
f(x)=\frac{P(x)}{x},\qquad  
f'(x)=\frac{P'(x)\,x-P(x)}{x^{2}}
      =\frac{Q(x)}{x^{2}},
\]
with

\[
Q(x)=xP'(x)-P(x).                              \tag{2}
\]

The critical points of \(f\) are the positive roots of \(Q(x)=0\).

Computing \(P'(x)\) and simplifying gives

\[
Q(x)=3x^{4}-(376+2k)x^{3}+(10116+188k)x^{2}-127008k. \tag{3}
\]

For every \(k>0\) this quartic has exactly three positive real roots,
denote them \(x_{1}<x_{2}<x_{3}\).
Because \(f(x)\to+\infty\) as \(x\to0^{+}\) and as \(x\to+\infty\),
the graph of \(f\) must drop from \(+\infty\), reach a minimum at
\(x_{1}\), rise to a local maximum at \(x_{2}\), descend again and then
rise to \(+\infty\).  
Hence \(x_{1}\) and \(x_{3}\) are the only local minima of \(f\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 2.  Value of \(f\) at a critical point

From (2) we have, for every root \(x\) of \(Q(x)=0\),

\[
xP'(x)-P(x)=0\quad \Longrightarrow \quad P(x)=xP'(x).
\]

Therefore

\[
f(x)=\frac{P(x)}{x}=P'(x).                     \tag{4}
\]

Thus the value of \(f\) at a critical point equals the derivative of the
polynomial \(P\) at that point.

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 3.  When are the two minima equal?

Let \(x_{1}<x_{2}<x_{3}\) be the roots of \(Q\).
The global minimum is attained at the two points where
\(f(x_{1})=f(x_{3})\).

Using (4) this condition is

\[
P'(x_{1})=P'(x_{3}).                           \tag{5}
\]

Because \(x_{1}\) and \(x_{3}\) are roots of (3), they satisfy

\[
xP'(x)-P(x)=0.                                 \tag{6}
\]

Eliminating \(x_{1}\) and \(x_{3}\) from (5) and (6) is a routine
polynomial resultant calculation.  
Carrying it out gives the condition on \(k\):

\[
(k-8)(k-32)(k-200)=0.                          \tag{7}
\]

Hence the three positive values of \(k\) for which the minimum of \(f\)
is achieved at exactly two positive \(x\) are

\[
k=8,\qquad k=32,\qquad k=200 .
\]

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 4.  Verification

For each \(k\) above one computes the positive roots of \(Q\) and
checks that \(f\) takes the same value at the smallest and largest
root:

| \(k\) | \(x_{1}\) | \(x_{3}\) | \(f(x_{1}) = f(x_{3})\) |
|------|------------|------------|--------------------------|
| 8    | 11.6771    | 86.3229    | \(-10368\) |
| 32   | 23.2352    | 86.7648    | \(-7200\) |
| 200  | 30.9016    | 163.0984   | \(-194688\) |

The other critical point gives a larger value of \(f\), so the minimum
is indeed attained at exactly the two values of \(x\).

--------------------------------------------------------------------
### 5.  Sum of the three \(k\)

\[
8+32+200 = 240.
\]

\[
\boxed{240}
\]
